V3-081 | Specialty Beverage, Draft & High-Control Bar Operations

Draft Beer Line Cleaning Verification SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for draft beer line cleaning verification so specialty operations do not
depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Bartenders, barbacks, beverage managers, trained service employees, and maintenance/vendor personnel authorized for beverage
systems.

The beverage manager or shift leader owns product, sanitation, equipment, and quality standards. Only trained employees or approved
vendors perform restricted maintenance or gas-system work.

Whenever this specialty operation is performed and at each required monitoring, handoff, verification, or closeout point defined by the
restaurant.

• Current beverage recipe/process standards

• Approved cleaning and sanitation supplies

• Manufacturer instructions and authorized service contacts

• Labels, batch/date records, and quality-control logs

• Required PPE, tools, gas-detection or safety equipment where applicable

• Beverage systems are operated, cleaned, and maintained according to manufacturer instructions and approved restaurant procedures.

• Gas cylinders and pressurized systems are secured, handled, and serviced only by trained/authorized personnel.

• Batch beverages are identified, dated, stored, and discarded according to the approved process.

• Quality failures, leaks, sanitation concerns, pressure problems, or unsafe equipment require immediate stop-use and manager escalation.

1. Identify the exact equipment/system involved in draft beer line cleaning verification and review current manufacturer instructions, service history,
hazards, and employee authorization limits.

2. Inspect the surrounding area for food, guest, electrical, gas, fire, chemical, pressure, slip/fall, or contamination hazards and control access as needed.

3. Remove affected equipment from service, isolate energy/product, post signage, or activate the approved emergency control when the condition
requires it.

4. Gather the approved tools/PPE and contact an authorized service provider whenever the work exceeds normal trained-operator duties.

5. Perform or coordinate draft beer line cleaning verification according to the controlled maintenance/service procedure; never bypass guards, interlocks,
alarms, ventilation, or safety devices.

6. Record findings, measurements, service performed, parts/replenishment, vendor identity, and any condition that can recur.

7. Clean/sanitize any food-contact or exposed area affected by the work and remove tools, debris, chemicals, and temporary barriers appropriately.

8. Test normal operation and all relevant safety, alarm, drainage, ventilation, temperature, pressure, or utility functions before return to service.

9. If the system cannot be verified safe and functional, keep it out of service and implement the approved operational workaround or closure decision.

10. The manager signs the service record, updates preventive-maintenance needs, and communicates any operating restriction to affected employees.

■ Draft Beer Line Cleaning Verification was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.


■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

10. The manager signs the service record, updates preventive-maintenance needs, and communicates any operating restriction to affected employees.

V3-097 | Uncommon Equipment, Utilities & Facility Systems

Ice Machine Contamination Shutdown and Recommissioning SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for ice machine contamination shutdown and recommissioning so specialty
operations do not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Facilities leaders, managers, maintenance employees, authorized vendors, and employees affected by utility or equipment controls.

The facilities owner or manager on duty controls access, vendor coordination, shutdown/restart, and records. Employees do not bypass
safety devices or perform work beyond their training or authorization.

At the end of the applicable service, event, route, shift, equipment cycle, or temporary operation, before the responsible employee is
released.

• Manufacturer/service documentation

• Preventive-maintenance and inspection records

• Lockout/signage or area-control materials as applicable

• Authorized vendor and emergency contact list

• Verification checklist and post-service record

• Only trained employees or authorized service providers perform technical maintenance beyond normal operator tasks.

• Equipment or utilities with a suspected safety, sanitation, fire, electrical, gas, or contamination hazard are removed from service or isolated until cleared.

• Required inspections and service records are retained according to company and local requirements.

• Restart occurs only after the affected area is clean, safe, functional, and verified by the accountable manager.

1. Identify the exact equipment/system involved in ice machine contamination shutdown and recommissioning and review current manufacturer
instructions, service history, hazards, and employee authorization limits.

2. Inspect the surrounding area for food, guest, electrical, gas, fire, chemical, pressure, slip/fall, or contamination hazards and control access as needed.

3. Remove affected equipment from service, isolate energy/product, post signage, or activate the approved emergency control when the condition
requires it.

4. Gather the approved tools/PPE and contact an authorized service provider whenever the work exceeds normal trained-operator duties.

5. Perform or coordinate ice machine contamination shutdown and recommissioning according to the controlled maintenance/service procedure; never
bypass guards, interlocks, alarms, ventilation, or safety devices.

6. Record findings, measurements, service performed, parts/replenishment, vendor identity, and any condition that can recur.

7. Clean/sanitize any food-contact or exposed area affected by the work and remove tools, debris, chemicals, and temporary barriers appropriately.

8. Test normal operation and all relevant safety, alarm, drainage, ventilation, temperature, pressure, or utility functions before return to service.

9. If the system cannot be verified safe and functional, keep it out of service and implement the approved operational workaround or closure decision.

10. The manager signs the service record, updates preventive-maintenance needs, and communicates any operating restriction to affected employees.

■ Ice Machine Contamination Shutdown and Recommissioning was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.


■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

V3-101 | Advanced Disaster & Regional Continuity

Floodwater Intrusion Assessment and Sanitization SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for floodwater intrusion assessment and sanitization so specialty
operations do not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Owners, general managers, managers on duty, facilities leaders, food-safety leaders, and employees assigned to emergency
continuity roles.

The senior manager present activates the response, protects people first, coordinates authorities/vendors, controls product and
building decisions, and documents reopening authorization.

Immediately when the condition is suspected or confirmed, and continuously until the manager documents safe stabilization, recovery,
or closure.

• Emergency contact and authority list

• Business-continuity and closure/reopening checklist

• Employee accountability roster

• Food/product hold and disposition records

• Flashlights, radios, PPE, barriers, emergency water/power supplies, or other site-specific emergency equipment

• Life safety and official emergency instructions override normal operations.

• The restaurant closes, limits access, or suspends affected operations when safe food, water, utilities, air quality, sanitation, or building conditions
cannot be maintained.

• Potentially affected food, water, equipment, and surfaces are placed under control until evaluated.

• Reopening requires documented verification that critical utilities, sanitation, food safety, staffing, and building conditions are acceptable.

1. Treat floodwater intrusion assessment and sanitization as an abnormal condition and immediately determine whether people, food, water, utilities, air
quality, access, or the building are in immediate danger.

2. Call emergency services, utilities, property management, health/fire authorities, or other official resources when the event requires external direction.

3. Stop or restrict affected operations, keep employees/guests away from the hazard, and establish an accountable incident leader and communication
method.

4. Identify potentially affected food, water, equipment, documents, cash/data, and building areas; label/hold or secure them from use until evaluated.

5. Execute the approved response for floodwater intrusion assessment and sanitization and follow official instructions even when that requires closure,
evacuation, sheltering, menu reduction, or disposal.

6. Account for employees and communicate schedule/access instructions; document important decisions, times, conditions, photos, readings, and
authority/vendor contacts.

7. Do not reopen or restore affected food/equipment merely because the visible hazard has passed; verify sanitation, utilities, structure, food safety, and
equipment function.

8. Complete required cleaning, disinfection, disposal, repair, flushing, pest control, testing, or professional remediation before recommissioning.

9. Obtain manager/authority approval required by the applicable event and communicate any temporary restriction or reduced operating scope to the
team.

10. Conduct a post-incident review, close employee/guest/vendor follow-up, preserve records, and update continuity controls based on what failed or
worked.


■ Floodwater Intrusion Assessment and Sanitization was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Applicable permit, regulatory, certification, manufacturer, event, or company requirement: ______________________

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

Record future changes in the restaurant master SOP index, retain required evidence, and retrain affected employees whenever the approved process,

• Emergency contact and authority list

• Business-continuity and closure/reopening checklist

• Employee accountability roster

• Food/product hold and disposition records

• Flashlights, radios, PPE, barriers, emergency water/power supplies, or other site-specific emergency equipment

• Life safety and official emergency instructions override normal operations.

• The restaurant closes, limits access, or suspends affected operations when safe food, water, utilities, air quality, sanitation, or building conditions
cannot be maintained.

• Potentially affected food, water, equipment, and surfaces are placed under control until evaluated.

• Reopening requires documented verification that critical utilities, sanitation, food safety, staffing, and building conditions are acceptable.

1. Treat regional refrigeration grid failure product triage as an abnormal condition and immediately determine whether people, food, water, utilities, air
quality, access, or the building are in immediate danger.

2. Call emergency services, utilities, property management, health/fire authorities, or other official resources when the event requires external direction.

3. Stop or restrict affected operations, keep employees/guests away from the hazard, and establish an accountable incident leader and communication
method.

4. Identify potentially affected food, water, equipment, documents, cash/data, and building areas; label/hold or secure them from use until evaluated.

5. Execute the approved response for regional refrigeration grid failure product triage and follow official instructions even when that requires closure,
evacuation, sheltering, menu reduction, or disposal.

6. Account for employees and communicate schedule/access instructions; document important decisions, times, conditions, photos, readings, and
authority/vendor contacts.

7. Do not reopen or restore affected food/equipment merely because the visible hazard has passed; verify sanitation, utilities, structure, food safety, and
equipment function.

8. Complete required cleaning, disinfection, disposal, repair, flushing, pest control, testing, or professional remediation before recommissioning.

9. Obtain manager/authority approval required by the applicable event and communicate any temporary restriction or reduced operating scope to the
team.

10. Conduct a post-incident review, close employee/guest/vendor follow-up, preserve records, and update continuity controls based on what failed or
worked.


■ Regional Refrigeration Grid Failure Product Triage was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

V3-111 | Food Donation, Sustainability & Reusable Programs

Food Donation Eligibility and Segregation SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for food donation eligibility and segregation so specialty operations do not
depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Kitchen, food-safety, inventory, receiving, dish, sustainability, and manager teams responsible for donation, waste diversion, recycling,
oil, or reusable-container programs.

The assigned program owner verifies eligibility, segregation, sanitation, vendor/agency handoff, and records. The manager resolves
any food-safety, contamination, vendor, or documentation concern.

Whenever this specialty operation is performed and at each required monitoring, handoff, verification, or closeout point defined by the
restaurant.

• Approved program criteria and vendor/agency agreement

• Dedicated labeled containers or storage area

• Temperature/condition records when food safety applies

• Pickup/weight/diversion or transfer log

• Cleaning and sanitation supplies for reusable-program equipment

• Approved recipient-agency criteria and transfer documentation

• Donation and reuse programs never override food-safety requirements or approved disposition rules.

• Materials are clearly segregated so food, chemicals, waste, oil, recyclables, and reusable containers cannot cross-contaminate.

• Third-party handoffs are documented to the level required by the restaurant’s program and applicable rules.

• Unclear eligibility, contamination, damaged reusable items, or missed controls require hold/disposal rather than informal release.

1. Confirm the approved program criteria, participating vendor/agency, eligible material/product, responsible employee, and required record for food
donation eligibility and segregation.

2. Verify that food-safety, sanitation, chemical, waste, oil, packaging, and cross-contamination controls take priority over diversion or sustainability goals.

3. Inspect dedicated containers/storage and labels; remove anything damaged, contaminated, leaking, unidentified, or outside program criteria.

4. Segregate eligible items at the point of generation so they do not become mixed with trash, chemicals, allergens, unsafe food, or non-approved
material.

5. Perform food donation eligibility and segregation using the approved storage, temperature, sanitation, handling, pickup, or return method.

6. Record quantities/weights, condition, temperatures where applicable, date/time, responsible employee, and receiving vendor/agency or disposition.

7. Stop the handoff or reuse when eligibility, food safety, sanitation, container condition, vendor authorization, or documentation cannot be verified.

8. Clean and restore the collection/storage area after each handoff and control pests, spills, odor, leaks, and employee/guest access.

9. Reconcile missed pickups, rejected material, waste, contamination, or program exceptions and document corrective action.

10. The program owner reviews results and vendor/agency performance and adjusts pars, pickup frequency, training, or approved materials as needed.

■ Food Donation Eligibility and Segregation was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.


■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Applicable permit, regulatory, certification, manufacturer, event, or company requirement: ______________________

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

Record future changes in the restaurant master SOP index, retain required evidence, and retrain affected employees whenever the approved process,


V3-112 | Food Donation, Sustainability & Reusable Programs

Food Donation Cooling, Storage and Handoff SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for food donation cooling, storage and handoff so specialty operations do
not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Kitchen, food-safety, inventory, receiving, dish, sustainability, and manager teams responsible for donation, waste diversion, recycling,
oil, or reusable-container programs.

The assigned program owner verifies eligibility, segregation, sanitation, vendor/agency handoff, and records. The manager resolves
any food-safety, contamination, vendor, or documentation concern.

For every applicable receipt, transfer, pickup, release, or custody handoff, before the product/order is accepted or released.

• Approved program criteria and vendor/agency agreement

• Dedicated labeled containers or storage area

• Temperature/condition records when food safety applies

• Pickup/weight/diversion or transfer log

• Cleaning and sanitation supplies for reusable-program equipment

• Calibrated probe thermometer and approved cold-holding/transport equipment

• Approved recipient-agency criteria and transfer documentation

• Donation and reuse programs never override food-safety requirements or approved disposition rules.

• Materials are clearly segregated so food, chemicals, waste, oil, recyclables, and reusable containers cannot cross-contaminate.

• Third-party handoffs are documented to the level required by the restaurant’s program and applicable rules.

• Unclear eligibility, contamination, damaged reusable items, or missed controls require hold/disposal rather than informal release.

1. Confirm the approved program criteria, participating vendor/agency, eligible material/product, responsible employee, and required record for food
donation cooling, storage and handoff.

2. Verify that food-safety, sanitation, chemical, waste, oil, packaging, and cross-contamination controls take priority over diversion or sustainability goals.

3. Inspect dedicated containers/storage and labels; remove anything damaged, contaminated, leaking, unidentified, or outside program criteria.

4. Segregate eligible items at the point of generation so they do not become mixed with trash, chemicals, allergens, unsafe food, or non-approved
material.

5. Perform food donation cooling, storage and handoff using the approved storage, temperature, sanitation, handling, pickup, or return method.

6. Record quantities/weights, condition, temperatures where applicable, date/time, responsible employee, and receiving vendor/agency or disposition.

7. Stop the handoff or reuse when eligibility, food safety, sanitation, container condition, vendor authorization, or documentation cannot be verified.

8. Clean and restore the collection/storage area after each handoff and control pests, spills, odor, leaks, and employee/guest access.

9. Reconcile missed pickups, rejected material, waste, contamination, or program exceptions and document corrective action.

10. The program owner reviews results and vendor/agency performance and adjusts pars, pickup frequency, training, or approved materials as needed.

■ Food Donation Cooling, Storage and Handoff was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.


■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

• Cleaning and sanitation supplies for reusable-program equipment

• Approved recipient-agency criteria and transfer documentation

• Donation and reuse programs never override food-safety requirements or approved disposition rules.

• Materials are clearly segregated so food, chemicals, waste, oil, recyclables, and reusable containers cannot cross-contaminate.

• Third-party handoffs are documented to the level required by the restaurant’s program and applicable rules.

• Unclear eligibility, contamination, damaged reusable items, or missed controls require hold/disposal rather than informal release.

1. Confirm the approved program criteria, participating vendor/agency, eligible material/product, responsible employee, and required record for donation
agency pickup verification.

2. Verify that food-safety, sanitation, chemical, waste, oil, packaging, and cross-contamination controls take priority over diversion or sustainability goals.

3. Inspect dedicated containers/storage and labels; remove anything damaged, contaminated, leaking, unidentified, or outside program criteria.

4. Segregate eligible items at the point of generation so they do not become mixed with trash, chemicals, allergens, unsafe food, or non-approved
material.

5. Perform donation agency pickup verification using the approved storage, temperature, sanitation, handling, pickup, or return method.

6. Record quantities/weights, condition, temperatures where applicable, date/time, responsible employee, and receiving vendor/agency or disposition.

7. Stop the handoff or reuse when eligibility, food safety, sanitation, container condition, vendor authorization, or documentation cannot be verified.

8. Clean and restore the collection/storage area after each handoff and control pests, spills, odor, leaks, and employee/guest access.

9. Reconcile missed pickups, rejected material, waste, contamination, or program exceptions and document corrective action.

10. The program owner reviews results and vendor/agency performance and adjusts pars, pickup frequency, training, or approved materials as needed.

■ Donation Agency Pickup Verification was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.


■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

V3-121 | Large Events, Seasonal Operations & Crowd Management

Festival or Concert Demand Surge Readiness SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for festival or concert demand surge readiness so specialty operations do
not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Managers, event leaders, kitchen/service leads, security or host leaders, and employees assigned to surge, seasonal, patio, group, or
special-event operations.

The manager on duty owns the event/surge plan, staffing, capacity, safety, and service adjustments. Department leaders execute
assigned controls and report deviations immediately.

Before the applicable operation, service period, event, route, batch, opening, or equipment use, and again after any material condition
changes.

• Event or demand forecast and staffing plan

• Capacity, seating, queue, production, and staging plan

• Weather/site monitoring resources when applicable

• Communication devices and emergency contacts

• Event closeout, variance, and incident records

• Capacity, staffing, production, guest flow, and safety controls are set before the surge begins.

• Weather, crowd, group, and special-access risks are actively monitored rather than treated as normal-volume conditions.

• Managers reduce menu, seating, service scope, or operating area before demand exceeds safe control.

• Event changes and post-event lessons are documented for the next comparable event.

1. Confirm the current approved scope for festival or concert demand surge readiness, including date/time, location, guest count or demand assumption,
responsible leader, and special commitments.

2. Review staffing, production, equipment, transport/site, payment, guest-access, safety, and food-safety requirements; assign an owner to each critical
control.

3. Inspect the required area, equipment, supplies, communication method, and backup resources before product or guests arrive.

4. Stage and label materials in the sequence they will be used; separate guest-specific, event-specific, allergen, payment, and high-risk items as
applicable.

5. Execute festival or concert demand surge readiness using the current event/service plan and verify critical handoffs before proceeding to the next
phase.

6. Monitor volume, staffing, timing, food condition, guest flow, and safety; record exceptions and adjust before control is lost.

7. Escalate shortages, unsafe conditions, late changes, quality failures, or guest commitments the team cannot meet to the event manager immediately.

8. Complete the guest/client or internal handoff, confirm unresolved items, and document any approved change to the original plan.

9. Reconcile product, equipment, payments, damage, waste, incidents, and outstanding follow-up; clean and restore the work/site area.

10. The accountable manager reviews the record, closes open issues, and captures lessons that should change the next comparable event.

■ Festival or Concert Demand Surge Readiness was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.


■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Applicable permit, regulatory, certification, manufacturer, event, or company requirement: ______________________

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

Record future changes in the restaurant master SOP index, retain required evidence, and retrain affected employees whenever the approved process,

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Applicable permit, regulatory, certification, manufacturer, event, or company requirement: ______________________

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

V3-130 | Large Events, Seasonal Operations & Crowd Management

Late-Night Special Event Closeout SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for late-night special event closeout so specialty operations do not depend
on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Managers, event leaders, kitchen/service leads, security or host leaders, and employees assigned to surge, seasonal, patio, group, or
special-event operations.

The manager on duty owns the event/surge plan, staffing, capacity, safety, and service adjustments. Department leaders execute
assigned controls and report deviations immediately.

At the end of the applicable service, event, route, shift, equipment cycle, or temporary operation, before the responsible employee is
released.

• Event or demand forecast and staffing plan

• Capacity, seating, queue, production, and staging plan

• Weather/site monitoring resources when applicable

• Communication devices and emergency contacts

• Event closeout, variance, and incident records

• Capacity, staffing, production, guest flow, and safety controls are set before the surge begins.

• Weather, crowd, group, and special-access risks are actively monitored rather than treated as normal-volume conditions.

• Managers reduce menu, seating, service scope, or operating area before demand exceeds safe control.

• Event changes and post-event lessons are documented for the next comparable event.

1. Confirm the current approved scope for late-night special event closeout, including date/time, location, guest count or demand assumption, responsible
leader, and special commitments.

2. Review staffing, production, equipment, transport/site, payment, guest-access, safety, and food-safety requirements; assign an owner to each critical
control.

3. Inspect the required area, equipment, supplies, communication method, and backup resources before product or guests arrive.

4. Stage and label materials in the sequence they will be used; separate guest-specific, event-specific, allergen, payment, and high-risk items as
applicable.

5. Execute late-night special event closeout using the current event/service plan and verify critical handoffs before proceeding to the next phase.

6. Monitor volume, staffing, timing, food condition, guest flow, and safety; record exceptions and adjust before control is lost.

7. Escalate shortages, unsafe conditions, late changes, quality failures, or guest commitments the team cannot meet to the event manager immediately.

8. Complete the guest/client or internal handoff, confirm unresolved items, and document any approved change to the original plan.

9. Reconcile product, equipment, payments, damage, waste, incidents, and outstanding follow-up; clean and restore the work/site area.

10. The accountable manager reviews the record, closes open issues, and captures lessons that should change the next comparable event.

■ Late-Night Special Event Closeout was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.


■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Applicable permit, regulatory, certification, manufacturer, event, or company requirement: ______________________

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

Record future changes in the restaurant master SOP index, retain required evidence, and retrain affected employees whenever the approved process,


V3-131 | Multi-Unit Governance, Openings & Temporary Leadership

New Unit Pre-Opening Operational Readiness SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for new unit pre-opening operational readiness so specialty operations do
not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Owners, regional leaders, general managers, opening teams, temporary managers, trainers, auditors, and unit leaders.

The designated regional or ownership authority defines decision rights and approves exceptions. Unit managers execute the current
controlled standard and document deviations or temporary authority.

Before the applicable operation, service period, event, route, batch, opening, or equipment use, and again after any material condition
changes.

• Current controlled SOP/index and deployment record

• Opening, handoff, or transition checklist

• Authority matrix and escalation contacts

• Audit/calibration forms and exception record

• Training, acknowledgment, and implementation evidence

• Every unit can identify the current approved version of a standard and who may authorize exceptions.

• Temporary leadership authority is written, time-bounded, and communicated before decisions are delegated.

• New or acquired units do not claim readiness until critical people, systems, records, food-safety, cash, facility, and opening controls are verified.

• Cross-unit audits use the same definitions and evidence standards so results are comparable.

1. Define the scope, effective date, accountable leader, participating units, and decision authority for new unit pre-opening operational readiness.

2. Verify the current controlled standard, version, required forms, training evidence, system dependencies, and any legal/permit constraints before
implementation.

3. Document temporary or permanent authority, exceptions, transition risks, unresolved liabilities, and escalation paths in writing.

4. Brief affected managers and employees on what changes, what does not change, who owns decisions, and what evidence proves adoption.

5. Execute new unit pre-opening operational readiness according to the approved deployment/transition plan and prevent local workarounds from silently
replacing the controlled standard.

6. Verify implementation through records, observation, audit evidence, training sign-off, and manager acknowledgment—not verbal assurance alone.

7. Log exceptions and require the authorized owner to approve, time-limit, and review any deviation from the system standard.

8. Calibrate findings across units/leaders so identical conditions are scored and corrected consistently.

9. Close transition/opening/coverage actions with evidence and transfer unresolved items to the named owner and due date.

10. The regional/ownership authority records the final decision and updates controlled standards or support resources when the transition reveals a
systemic gap.

■ New Unit Pre-Opening Operational Readiness was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.


■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Applicable permit, regulatory, certification, manufacturer, event, or company requirement: ______________________

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

8. Calibrate findings across units/leaders so identical conditions are scored and corrected consistently.

9. Close transition/opening/coverage actions with evidence and transfer unresolved items to the named owner and due date.

10. The regional/ownership authority records the final decision and updates controlled standards or support resources when the transition reveals a
systemic gap.

■ Cross-Unit Audit Calibration was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.


■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

Extended Unit Mothballing and Restart SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for extended unit mothballing and restart so specialty operations do not
depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Owners, regional leaders, general managers, opening teams, temporary managers, trainers, auditors, and unit leaders.

The designated regional or ownership authority defines decision rights and approves exceptions. Unit managers execute the current
controlled standard and document deviations or temporary authority.

Whenever this specialty operation is performed and at each required monitoring, handoff, verification, or closeout point defined by the
restaurant.

• Current controlled SOP/index and deployment record

• Opening, handoff, or transition checklist

• Authority matrix and escalation contacts

• Audit/calibration forms and exception record

• Training, acknowledgment, and implementation evidence

• Every unit can identify the current approved version of a standard and who may authorize exceptions.

• Temporary leadership authority is written, time-bounded, and communicated before decisions are delegated.

• New or acquired units do not claim readiness until critical people, systems, records, food-safety, cash, facility, and opening controls are verified.

• Cross-unit audits use the same definitions and evidence standards so results are comparable.

1. Define the scope, effective date, accountable leader, participating units, and decision authority for extended unit mothballing and restart.

2. Verify the current controlled standard, version, required forms, training evidence, system dependencies, and any legal/permit constraints before
implementation.

3. Document temporary or permanent authority, exceptions, transition risks, unresolved liabilities, and escalation paths in writing.

4. Brief affected managers and employees on what changes, what does not change, who owns decisions, and what evidence proves adoption.

5. Execute extended unit mothballing and restart according to the approved deployment/transition plan and prevent local workarounds from silently
replacing the controlled standard.

6. Verify implementation through records, observation, audit evidence, training sign-off, and manager acknowledgment—not verbal assurance alone.

7. Log exceptions and require the authorized owner to approve, time-limit, and review any deviation from the system standard.

8. Calibrate findings across units/leaders so identical conditions are scored and corrected consistently.

9. Close transition/opening/coverage actions with evidence and transfer unresolved items to the named owner and due date.

10. The regional/ownership authority records the final decision and updates controlled standards or support resources when the transition reveals a
systemic gap.

V3-141 | Technology, Digital Ordering & Data Continuity

Online Ordering Menu Sync Verification SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for online ordering menu sync verification so specialty operations do not
depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Managers, POS/digital-ordering administrators, authorized technology support, cash-control leaders, and employees using restaurant
technology.

The designated system owner controls access, approved configuration, backups, incident escalation, and restoration. Employees use
only authorized accounts/devices and immediately report suspicious activity or failure.

Whenever this specialty operation is performed and at each required monitoring, handoff, verification, or closeout point defined by the
restaurant.

• Current system inventory and authorized-access list

• Vendor support and incident contacts

• Backup/offline procedures and critical paper forms

• Change, outage, incident, and restoration log

• Approved security, network, payment, and device-inspection guidance

• Access is limited to authorized users and shared credentials are avoided where the system supports individual accounts.

• Operational and guest networks, payment systems, backups, and connected equipment follow current vendor/company security controls.

• Suspected payment-device tampering or data compromise triggers immediate stop-use, preservation of evidence, and escalation to the appropriate
payment/vendor/security contacts.

• Restoration is verified before normal dependence resumes, and critical records remain available through an approved offline method.

1. Identify the system/device/data involved in online ordering menu sync verification, its business owner, authorized users, vendor/support contact, and
approved fallback method.

2. Confirm current access rights, device inventory, network/payment boundaries, backup status, support credentials, and required incident records before
making changes.

3. Inspect devices/connections for unexpected changes, tampering, unsupported software, alarms, failed synchronization, or other signs of compromise
or loss of control.

4. Execute online ordering menu sync verification using only approved accounts, devices, settings, networks, storage locations, and vendor/company
procedures.

5. Protect credentials and payment/guest/employee data; do not share passwords, disable security controls, or move protected data into unapproved
personal tools.

6. Record outage/change/inspection time, affected systems, observed condition, actions taken, vendor/ticket information, and any data or operational
impact.

7. On suspected tampering, breach, unauthorized access, or unexplained data loss, stop the affected activity as appropriate and preserve evidence for
the authorized incident owner.

8. Activate the approved offline/manual backup process so critical operations and records continue without bypassing required cash, food-safety, or
authorization controls.

9. Restore or reconnect only after the system owner verifies configuration, security, data integrity/synchronization, and required tests.

10. Close the incident/change record, rotate or revoke access if required, verify backups, and document prevention actions for recurring failures.


■ Online Ordering Menu Sync Verification was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

9. Restore or reconnect only after the system owner verifies configuration, security, data integrity/synchronization, and required tests.


10. Close the incident/change record, rotate or revoke access if required, verify backups, and document prevention actions for recurring failures.

V3-146 | Technology, Digital Ordering & Data Continuity

Suspected Payment Card Data Breach Escalation SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for suspected payment card data breach escalation so specialty operations
do not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Managers, POS/digital-ordering administrators, authorized technology support, cash-control leaders, and employees using restaurant
technology.

The designated system owner controls access, approved configuration, backups, incident escalation, and restoration. Employees use
only authorized accounts/devices and immediately report suspicious activity or failure.

Immediately when the condition is suspected or confirmed, and continuously until the manager documents safe stabilization, recovery,
or closure.

• Current system inventory and authorized-access list

• Vendor support and incident contacts

• Backup/offline procedures and critical paper forms

• Change, outage, incident, and restoration log

• Approved security, network, payment, and device-inspection guidance

• Current payment-provider and PCI security guidance

• Approved access-control, backup, recovery, and cybersecurity procedures

• Access is limited to authorized users and shared credentials are avoided where the system supports individual accounts.

• Operational and guest networks, payment systems, backups, and connected equipment follow current vendor/company security controls.

• Suspected payment-device tampering or data compromise triggers immediate stop-use, preservation of evidence, and escalation to the appropriate
payment/vendor/security contacts.

• Restoration is verified before normal dependence resumes, and critical records remain available through an approved offline method.

• Payment devices are periodically inspected for tampering or substitution under the merchant’s current payment-security program; suspicious devices
are removed from use and escalated.

• Do not investigate a suspected data breach by deleting logs, rebooting systems, or destroying evidence unless directed by the authorized
incident-response owner.

1. Treat any credible sign of payment-card data compromise as a security incident: stop avoidable exposure, notify the authorized incident
owner/provider, and preserve devices, logs, timestamps, and other evidence.

2. Confirm current access rights, device inventory, network/payment boundaries, backup status, support credentials, and required incident records before
making changes.

3. Inspect devices/connections for unexpected changes, tampering, unsupported software, alarms, failed synchronization, or other signs of compromise
or loss of control.

4. Execute suspected payment card data breach escalation using only approved accounts, devices, settings, networks, storage locations, and
vendor/company procedures.

5. Protect credentials and payment/guest/employee data; do not share passwords, disable security controls, or move protected data into unapproved
personal tools.

6. Record outage/change/inspection time, affected systems, observed condition, actions taken, vendor/ticket information, and any data or operational
impact.

7. On suspected tampering, breach, unauthorized access, or unexplained data loss, stop the affected activity as appropriate and preserve evidence for
the authorized incident owner.


8. Activate the approved offline/manual backup process so critical operations and records continue without bypassing required cash, food-safety, or
authorization controls.

9. Restore or reconnect only after the system owner verifies configuration, security, data integrity/synchronization, and required tests.

10. Close the incident/change record, rotate or revoke access if required, verify backups, and document prevention actions for recurring failures.

To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for guest wi-fi and operations network separation so specialty operations
do not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

technology.

The designated system owner controls access, approved configuration, backups, incident escalation, and restoration. Employees use
only authorized accounts/devices and immediately report suspicious activity or failure.

Whenever this specialty operation is performed and at each required monitoring, handoff, verification, or closeout point defined by the
restaurant.

• Current system inventory and authorized-access list

• Vendor support and incident contacts

• Backup/offline procedures and critical paper forms

• Change, outage, incident, and restoration log

• Approved security, network, payment, and device-inspection guidance

• Approved access-control, backup, recovery, and cybersecurity procedures

• Access is limited to authorized users and shared credentials are avoided where the system supports individual accounts.

• Operational and guest networks, payment systems, backups, and connected equipment follow current vendor/company security controls.

• Suspected payment-device tampering or data compromise triggers immediate stop-use, preservation of evidence, and escalation to the appropriate
payment/vendor/security contacts.

• Restoration is verified before normal dependence resumes, and critical records remain available through an approved offline method.

1. Identify the system/device/data involved in guest wi-fi and operations network separation, its business owner, authorized users, vendor/support
contact, and approved fallback method.

2. Confirm current access rights, device inventory, network/payment boundaries, backup status, support credentials, and required incident records before
making changes.

3. Inspect devices/connections for unexpected changes, tampering, unsupported software, alarms, failed synchronization, or other signs of compromise
or loss of control.

4. Execute guest wi-fi and operations network separation using only approved accounts, devices, settings, networks, storage locations, and
vendor/company procedures.

5. Protect credentials and payment/guest/employee data; do not share passwords, disable security controls, or move protected data into unapproved
personal tools.

6. Record outage/change/inspection time, affected systems, observed condition, actions taken, vendor/ticket information, and any data or operational
impact.

7. On suspected tampering, breach, unauthorized access, or unexplained data loss, stop the affected activity as appropriate and preserve evidence for
the authorized incident owner.

8. Activate the approved offline/manual backup process so critical operations and records continue without bypassing required cash, food-safety, or
authorization controls.

9. Restore or reconnect only after the system owner verifies configuration, security, data integrity/synchronization, and required tests.

10. Close the incident/change record, rotate or revoke access if required, verify backups, and document prevention actions for recurring failures.

V3-151 | Grab-and-Go, Automated & Unattended Service

Grab-and-Go Packaged Food Label Verification SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for grab-and-go packaged food label verification so specialty operations do
not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Grab-and-go, retail, kitchen, expo, kiosk, locker, vending, and manager teams.

The assigned retail/off-premise owner controls labels, time/temperature, stocking, security, and removal. The manager verifies
exceptions, outages, abandoned orders, and product disposition.

Whenever this specialty operation is performed and at each required monitoring, handoff, verification, or closeout point defined by the
restaurant.

• Approved labels and product specifications

• Calibrated thermometers or automated monitoring where used

• Rotation, stocking, withdrawal, and disposition records

• Approved packaging and consumer instructions

• Kiosk/locker/vending access and support contacts

• Packaged products carry required identity, date, allergen, handling, and consumer information under the restaurant’s approved labeling program.

• Time/temperature-controlled products remain within approved conditions through display, locker, vending, or pickup.

• Expired, mislabeled, temperature-abused, damaged, recalled, or abandoned product is removed from sale/use immediately.

• Automated systems have a documented manual or shutdown response when monitoring, payment, accessibility, or release controls fail.

1. Confirm the approved product/service specification and assigned employee for grab-and-go packaged food label verification.

2. Review required labels, ingredients, equipment, monitoring tools, service/display limits, rotation method, and corrective-action rules.

3. Inspect and clean the station/equipment; verify barriers, utensils, containers, monitoring devices, and guest-protection controls are ready.

4. Stage product in controlled quantities, maintain required segregation, and attach identity/date/allergen or handling information before display/use.

5. Perform grab-and-go packaged food label verification using the approved operating sequence; do not mix uncontrolled old and new product or bypass
required monitoring.

6. Check product condition, time/temperature, labels, equipment, guest-contact risk, and sanitation at the restaurant-approved frequency and record
results.

7. Remove, hold, discard, or replace product immediately when a required control is missed, contamination is suspected, or identity cannot be verified.

8. Replenish only from approved protected stock and keep the station orderly so employees can see age, condition, and ownership of product.

9. At close/changeover, dispose or store product only under the approved rule, clean and sanitize the station/equipment, and complete records.

10. The manager reviews the monitoring record and recurring deviations before the next service period.

■ Grab-and-Go Packaged Food Label Verification was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.


• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

SOP

operations do not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

The assigned retail/off-premise owner controls labels, time/temperature, stocking, security, and removal. The manager verifies
exceptions, outages, abandoned orders, and product disposition.

For every applicable receipt, transfer, pickup, release, or custody handoff, before the product/order is accepted or released.

• Approved labels and product specifications

• Calibrated thermometers or automated monitoring where used

• Rotation, stocking, withdrawal, and disposition records

• Approved packaging and consumer instructions

• Kiosk/locker/vending access and support contacts

• Calibrated probe thermometer and approved cold-holding/transport equipment

• Approved system access, monitoring, accessibility, and outage procedures

• Packaged products carry required identity, date, allergen, handling, and consumer information under the restaurant’s approved labeling program.

• Time/temperature-controlled products remain within approved conditions through display, locker, vending, or pickup.

• Expired, mislabeled, temperature-abused, damaged, recalled, or abandoned product is removed from sale/use immediately.

• Automated systems have a documented manual or shutdown response when monitoring, payment, accessibility, or release controls fail.

1. Confirm the approved product/service specification and assigned employee for refrigerated pickup locker loading and temperature control.

2. Review required labels, ingredients, equipment, monitoring tools, service/display limits, rotation method, and corrective-action rules.

3. Inspect and clean the station/equipment; verify barriers, utensils, containers, monitoring devices, and guest-protection controls are ready.

4. Stage product in controlled quantities, maintain required segregation, and attach identity/date/allergen or handling information before display/use.

5. Perform refrigerated pickup locker loading and temperature control using the approved operating sequence; do not mix uncontrolled old and new
product or bypass required monitoring.

6. Check product condition, time/temperature, labels, equipment, guest-contact risk, and sanitation at the restaurant-approved frequency and record
results.

7. Remove, hold, discard, or replace product immediately when a required control is missed, contamination is suspected, or identity cannot be verified.

8. Replenish only from approved protected stock and keep the station orderly so employees can see age, condition, and ownership of product.

9. At close/changeover, dispose or store product only under the approved rule, clean and sanitize the station/equipment, and complete records.

10. The manager reviews the monitoring record and recurring deviations before the next service period.

To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for heated pickup locker holding and release so specialty operations do not
depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Grab-and-go, retail, kitchen, expo, kiosk, locker, vending, and manager teams.

The assigned retail/off-premise owner controls labels, time/temperature, stocking, security, and removal. The manager verifies
exceptions, outages, abandoned orders, and product disposition.

For every applicable receipt, transfer, pickup, release, or custody handoff, before the product/order is accepted or released.

• Approved labels and product specifications

• Calibrated thermometers or automated monitoring where used

• Rotation, stocking, withdrawal, and disposition records

• Approved packaging and consumer instructions

• Kiosk/locker/vending access and support contacts

• Calibrated probe thermometer and approved hot-holding/transport equipment

• Approved system access, monitoring, accessibility, and outage procedures

• Packaged products carry required identity, date, allergen, handling, and consumer information under the restaurant’s approved labeling program.

• Time/temperature-controlled products remain within approved conditions through display, locker, vending, or pickup.

• Expired, mislabeled, temperature-abused, damaged, recalled, or abandoned product is removed from sale/use immediately.

3. Inspect and clean the station/equipment; verify barriers, utensils, containers, monitoring devices, and guest-protection controls are ready.

4. Stage product in controlled quantities, maintain required segregation, and attach identity/date/allergen or handling information before display/use.

5. Perform heated pickup locker holding and release using the approved operating sequence; do not mix uncontrolled old and new product or bypass
required monitoring.

6. Check product condition, time/temperature, labels, equipment, guest-contact risk, and sanitation at the restaurant-approved frequency and record
results.

7. Remove, hold, discard, or replace product immediately when a required control is missed, contamination is suspected, or identity cannot be verified.

8. Replenish only from approved protected stock and keep the station orderly so employees can see age, condition, and ownership of product.

9. At close/changeover, dispose or store product only under the approved rule, clean and sanitize the station/equipment, and complete records.

10. The manager reviews the monitoring record and recurring deviations before the next service period.

V3-161 | Shared Kitchens, Pop-Ups & Remote Service Venues

Shared Commercial Kitchen Opening and Space Handoff SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for shared commercial kitchen opening and space handoff so specialty
operations do not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Managers, shared-kitchen users, pop-up/event teams, concession teams, room-service teams, and employees operating in borrowed,
remote, or multi-tenant spaces.

The designated operation lead owns the space handoff, segregation, temporary setup, service controls, and restoration. The manager
escalates facility, co-tenant, contamination, access, or authority issues.

Before the applicable operation, service period, event, route, batch, opening, or equipment use, and again after any material condition
changes.

• Site agreement, permit/event requirements, and approved operating plan

• Opening/closing and space-handoff checklist

• Dedicated labels, storage identifiers, smallwares, and chemical controls

• Food-safety monitoring and sanitation supplies

• Damage, contamination, incident, and site-restoration records

• The team verifies what space, equipment, storage, utilities, cleaning, and access are actually under its control before operating.

• Food, allergens, chemicals, smallwares, and storage are segregated from co-tenants or unrelated operations.

• Temporary service does not begin until handwashing, sanitation, food protection, utilities, and safe guest/employee access are established.

• The site is returned in the documented condition required by the agreement, with unresolved issues handed off in writing.

1. Confirm the written scope for shared commercial kitchen opening and space handoff, including site owner, available space/equipment/utilities,
operating times, access rules, permits, and restoration obligations.

2. Walk the site before setup and document cleanliness, damage, pest evidence, utilities, storage, handwashing, warewashing, refrigeration/hot holding,
waste, lighting, and safe access.

3. Assign and label the restaurant’s food, chemicals, smallwares, equipment, cold/dry storage, and personal items so ownership remains clear in the
shared/temporary environment.

4. Establish handwashing, sanitation, allergen, food-protection, waste, security, and employee/guest-flow controls before food preparation or service
begins.

5. Perform shared commercial kitchen opening and space handoff within the approved footprint and prevent co-tenant or venue activity from
contaminating, displacing, or compromising the restaurant’s operation.

6. Monitor utilities, storage, product condition, access, co-tenant activity, and sanitation throughout service and document any shared-space incident.

7. Stop or relocate the affected activity if safe food handling, sanitation, utilities, segregation, or site security cannot be maintained.

8. At handoff/closeout, reconcile food/equipment, remove waste and chemicals, clean/sanitize assigned areas, and restore the site as required.

9. Document damage, contamination, missing property, shared-storage disputes, or facility defects and notify the site owner/manager promptly.

10. The operation lead completes the space-handoff record and blocks the next use until unresolved critical defects are corrected.

■ Shared Commercial Kitchen Opening and Space Handoff was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.


■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.

Applicable permit, regulatory, certification, manufacturer, event, or company requirement: ______________________

Restaurant-specific critical limit, service target, frequency, or acceptance criterion: __________________________

Record future changes in the restaurant master SOP index, retain required evidence, and retrain affected employees whenever the approved process,


V3-162 | Shared Kitchens, Pop-Ups & Remote Service Venues

Shared Kitchen Allergen, Chemical and Smallware Segregation
SOP
To establish a controlled, repeatable, and verifiable process for shared kitchen allergen, chemical and smallware segregation so
specialty operations do not depend on improvisation, undocumented exceptions, or unassigned responsibility.

Managers, shared-kitchen users, pop-up/event teams, concession teams, room-service teams, and employees operating in borrowed,
remote, or multi-tenant spaces.

The designated operation lead owns the space handoff, segregation, temporary setup, service controls, and restoration. The manager
escalates facility, co-tenant, contamination, access, or authority issues.

Whenever this specialty operation is performed and at each required monitoring, handoff, verification, or closeout point defined by the
restaurant.

• Site agreement, permit/event requirements, and approved operating plan

• Opening/closing and space-handoff checklist

• Dedicated labels, storage identifiers, smallwares, and chemical controls

• Food-safety monitoring and sanitation supplies

• Damage, contamination, incident, and site-restoration records

• Current allergen/ingredient information and dedicated cross-contact controls

• The team verifies what space, equipment, storage, utilities, cleaning, and access are actually under its control before operating.

• Food, allergens, chemicals, smallwares, and storage are segregated from co-tenants or unrelated operations.

• Temporary service does not begin until handwashing, sanitation, food protection, utilities, and safe guest/employee access are established.

• The site is returned in the documented condition required by the agreement, with unresolved issues handed off in writing.

• Allergen or special-diet claims are never guessed; ingredient, cross-contact, and approved preparation controls are verified before service.

1. Confirm the written scope for shared kitchen allergen, chemical and smallware segregation, including site owner, available space/equipment/utilities,
operating times, access rules, permits, and restoration obligations.

2. Walk the site before setup and document cleanliness, damage, pest evidence, utilities, storage, handwashing, warewashing, refrigeration/hot holding,
waste, lighting, and safe access.

3. Assign and label the restaurant’s food, chemicals, smallwares, equipment, cold/dry storage, and personal items so ownership remains clear in the
shared/temporary environment.

4. Establish handwashing, sanitation, allergen, food-protection, waste, security, and employee/guest-flow controls before food preparation or service
begins.

5. Perform shared kitchen allergen, chemical and smallware segregation within the approved footprint and prevent co-tenant or venue activity from
contaminating, displacing, or compromising the restaurant’s operation.

6. Monitor utilities, storage, product condition, access, co-tenant activity, and sanitation throughout service and document any shared-space incident.

7. Stop or relocate the affected activity if safe food handling, sanitation, utilities, segregation, or site security cannot be maintained.

8. At handoff/closeout, reconcile food/equipment, remove waste and chemicals, clean/sanitize assigned areas, and restore the site as required.

9. Document damage, contamination, missing property, shared-storage disputes, or facility defects and notify the site owner/manager promptly.

10. The operation lead completes the space-handoff record and blocks the next use until unresolved critical defects are corrected.


■ Shared Kitchen Allergen, Chemical and Smallware Segregation was performed under the current approved restaurant procedure.

■ Required people, equipment, utilities, product, approvals, and records were verified before release or continued operation.

■ Critical readings, identities, handoffs, exceptions, or other evidence required by this procedure were recorded.

■ Unsafe, out-of-standard, unidentified, or undocumented conditions were held, stopped, or escalated instead of informally accepted.

■ Corrective actions and product/equipment/guest/employee disposition were completed and documented when required.

• Using an old, generic, or remembered procedure instead of the current approved specialty-operation standard.

• Starting before required permits, approvals, trained people, utilities, equipment, labels, or records are ready.

• Failing to document actual conditions, readings, identities, handoffs, or deviations at the time they occur.

• Treating a vendor, platform, event organizer, sister unit, or outside agency as responsible for controls that still belong to the restaurant.

• Closing the activity without manager verification, record completion, cleanup/restoration, and follow-up ownership.
